=\frac{1}{\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}(x))}\sum_{x_{j}\in{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}(x)}\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}.

We apply
eq.

3.29
in
eq.

3.28
and just study the sum over one
z^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,0}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,0}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{j}\in z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\left|\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})-\frac{1}{\mu(z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})}\sum_{x_{j^{\prime}}\in z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\nabla_{x_{j^{\prime}}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}.

Then we use the symmetry proved in
eq.

3.20
, that in fact every
\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}
has the same contribution for all
x_{j}\in z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,0}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,0}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{j}\in z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\left|\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})-\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})\right|^{2}\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}.

For the equation above, we can use the Poincaré inequality
Proposition

3.1
because it is centered and every
x_{i}
lives uniformly in its associated small cube
z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}
. We remark that the constant
C
here is independent of
N

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,0}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,0}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant C\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\sum_{x_{j}\in z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\left|\nabla_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})\right|^{2}\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}.

We put this estimate back to
eq.

3.28
, do the sum over all
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{z^{\prime}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,0}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,0}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant C\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N}\strokedint_{(z_{i}+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}}\left|\nabla_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x_{j}}w_{N}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}^{c})\right|^{2}\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}

\displaystyle\qquad=C\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})^{2}}\left|\nabla^{2}w(\mu,x,y)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x){\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,0}\right].

Finally, we do the expectation and the sum over all
{\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,0}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0})=N_{z}\right\}}}
, and use the
\mathscr{H}^{2}
-estimate
eq.

3.26
to obtain that

(3.30)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,0}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
&\qquad\leqslant C\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})^{2}}\left|\nabla^{2}w(\mu,x,y)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x){\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
&\qquad\leqslant C\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}],\end{split}

and this concludes that

(3.31)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:MultiDecom}-a}\leqslant C(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}])^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The term
eq.

3.27
-b can be treated similarly. For every
k
, we apply at first the conditional expectation with respect to
\mathcal{G}_{n,k}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}(\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w)\cdot\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}(\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w)\cdot\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,k}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}}\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}(\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w)\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

Then we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to obtain that

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}(\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w)\cdot\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}}\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}}\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We use the definition in
eq.

3.15
and Jensen’s inequality for
|\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w|^{2}
. For every
z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}
, since
(\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w)(\mu,z)
is
\mathcal{G}_{n,k}
-measurable,

(3.32)

\begin{split}|\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w|^{2}(\mu,z)&=\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}(\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,k}\right]\right)^{2}\\
&\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,k}\right].\end{split}

Then we sum over all
z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}
, and we can treat it like
eq.

3.27
-a and
eq.

3.30
with the Poincaré inequality in the scale
3^{k}
and the
\mathscr{H}^{2}
-estimate
eq.

3.26
, yielding

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,k}}\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\nabla w-\mathsf{S}_{n,k+1}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant C3^{2k}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}].

We have thus shown that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:MultiDecom}-b}\leqslant C(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}])^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}3^{k}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,k}\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

For
eq.

3.27
-c, we use
eq.

3.15
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to get that

eq.

3.27
-c

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}(\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w)\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

To treat the term
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]
, we define the random affine function

(3.34)

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}:=\frac{(\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w)(\mu,0)}{|(\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w)(\mu,0)|},\qquad\ell_{\mathsf{p},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}:=\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\mathsf{p}\cdot x\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x).

Notice that here
\mathsf{p}
is random, but when the particles in
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}
move within
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}
, it does mot change the value; more precisely, the slope
\mathsf{p}
is
\mathcal{G}_{n,n}
-measureable. We test
\ell_{\mathsf{p},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}
with
eq.

3.25
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[u\ell_{\mathsf{p},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla w\cdot\mathsf{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla w\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\cdot\mathsf{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}(\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w)\cdot\mathsf{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu.

Recalling the definition in
eq.

3.34
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w|\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[u\ell_{\mathsf{p},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[u^{2}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\ell^{2}_{\mathsf{p},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\sqrt{\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})}3^{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[u^{2}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where in the last step, we use a direct calculation of
\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\ell^{2}_{\mathsf{p},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and where the constant
C
may depend on
d
. Since
\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w
is constant for every point in
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}
, we have shown that

\sqrt{\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w|(\mu,0)\leqslant C3^{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[u^{2}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We thus obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla w|^{2}(\mu,0)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant C3^{2n}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[u^{2}\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{n,n}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=C3^{2n}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}],

and therefore

(3.35)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:MultiDecom}-c}\leqslant C3^{n}(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}])^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}|\mathsf{S}_{n,n}\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We now combine
eq.

3.27
, (
3.31
), (
3.33
), and (
3.35
), to obtain
eq.

3.23
.
∎

3.4.
Caccioppoli inequality

For every bounded open set
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we define the space of
\mathbf{a}
-harmonic functions on
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
by

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(U):=\left\{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U):\ \forall\varphi\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U),\ \mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0\right\}.

Recalling that, for any two bounded open sets
V\subseteq U
, we have
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)\subseteq\mathscr{H}^{1}(V)
and
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(V)\subseteq\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
, so we see that
\mathcal{A}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{A}(V)
.
For the classical Caccioppoli inequality, a standard proof is as follows: we multiply the harmonic function by a cutoff function, and then use this as a test function against the harmonic function itself. Adapting this argument to our space of particle configurations is not immediate. A naive approach would be to introduce a cutoff that brings the value of the function to zero whenever a particle approaches the boundary of the domain. But proceeding in this way is a very bad idea, since as we increase the size of the domain, there will essentially always be some particles near the boundary. We will instead rely on a suitable averaging procedure for particles that fall outside of a given region, using the localization operators defined in Subsection
2.3
. Notice that our goal thus is not to bring the function to zero as a particle approaches the boundary of the box. Rather, it is only to produce a function that stops depending on the position of a particle that progressively approaches the boundary of the domain, in agreement with our definition of the space
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
(and departing from the traditional definition of the Sobolev
H^{1}_{0}
spaces).

Proposition 3.9

(Modified Caccioppoli inequality)
.

There exist
{\theta(d,\Lambda)\in(0,1)}
,
{C(d,\Lambda)<\infty}
, and
{R_{0}(d,\Lambda)<\infty}
such that for every
r\geqslant R_{0}
and
{u\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{3r})}
, we have

(3.37)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{r}|}\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{r+2}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{r+2}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\frac{C}{r^{2}\rho|Q_{3r}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}]+\theta\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{3r}|}\int_{Q_{3r}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

Remark 3.10
.

Inequality
eq.

3.37
controls the norm of the gradient of a harmonic function in the small cube
Q_{r}
by a sum of terms involving the norm of the gradient in the larger cube
Q_{3r}
. This does not seem to be useful at first glance. However, the key point is that the multiplicative factor
\theta
is smaller than one.

The proof of Proposition
3.9
will be divided into two steps. In the first step, provided by the lemma below, we prove a weaker Caccioppoli inequality, without the normalization of the volume. In the second step we use an iterative argument to improve the result and obtain Proposition
3.9
.

Recall that
{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}
is the regularized localization operator defined in
eq.

2.9
.

Lemma 3.11

(Weak Caccioppoli inequality)
.

Fix
\theta^{\prime}(\Lambda):=\frac{2\Lambda}{2\Lambda+1}\in(0,1)
. For every
r>0
,
s\geqslant r+2,\varepsilon>0
and
{u\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{s+\varepsilon})}
, we have

(3.38)

\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\theta^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\varepsilon}u)^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right).

Proof.

The proof of this lemma borrows some elements from
[
41
, Lemma 4.8]
; in both settings, the main point is to construct and analyze an appropriate “cut-off” version of the function
u
. We use the function
{\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\varepsilon}u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(Q_{s+\varepsilon})}
defined in
eq.

2.13
as a cut-off of the function
u
and test it against
u\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{s+\varepsilon})
to get

(3.39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}}\nabla(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0.

Combining this with the decomposition

(3.40)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}}\nabla(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]&=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\nabla(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-a}}\\
&\qquad+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}\nabla(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-b}}\\
&\qquad+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}\nabla(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c}},\end{split}

we obtain that

(3.41)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-a}\leqslant|\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-b}|+|\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c}|.

We now study each of these three terms. For the first term
eq.

3.40
-a, since
x\in Q_{s-2}
, the coefficient
\mathbf{a}
is
\mathcal{F}_{Q_{s}}
-measurable. We can thus use
eq.

2.8
,
eq.

2.13
, (
2.15
) and (
2.14
) to get

eq.

3.40
-a

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon-t){\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon-t)\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\cdot\mathbf{a}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\,|\mathcal{F}_{\overline{Q}_{s+t}}\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We then apply
eq.

2.15
for the second term
eq.

3.40
-b. We notice that in
Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}
,
\mathbf{a}
is no longer
\mathcal{F}_{Q_{s}}
-measurable. So we use Young’s inequality and the bound
\mathbf{a}\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}

\displaystyle|\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-b}|

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon-t){\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon-t)\left(\left|{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\right|^{2}+\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

For the part with conditional expectation, we use Jensen’s inequality and the uniform bound
\mathsf{Id}\leqslant\mathbf{a}\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon-t)\left|{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

This concludes that
|\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-b}|\leqslant\Lambda\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}\backslash Q_{s-2}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]
.

For the third term
eq.

3.40
-c, we use
eq.

2.15
and obtain

\displaystyle|\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c1}+\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c2}

eq.

3.40
-c1

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}\int_{\tau(x)-s}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon-t){\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}(\nabla u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right|

eq.

3.40
-c2

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}(s+\varepsilon-\tau(x))\Delta_{\tau(x)}({\mathsf{A}}u)\overrightarrow{\mathbf{n}}(x)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right|.

The part of
eq.

3.40
-c1 can be treated as that of
eq.

3.40
-b, so that

\displaystyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c1}\leqslant\Lambda\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We study the part
eq.

3.40
-c2 using Young’s inequality with a parameter
\beta>0
to be fixed later:

(3.42)

\begin{split}&\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}(s+\varepsilon-\tau(x))\Delta_{\tau(x)}({\mathsf{A}}u)\overrightarrow{\mathbf{n}}(x)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right|\\
\leqslant&\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}(s+\varepsilon-\tau(x))|\Delta_{\tau(x)}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]+\frac{\beta\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}(s+\varepsilon-\tau(x))|\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant&\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}(s+\varepsilon-\tau(x))|\Delta_{\tau(x)}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]+\frac{\beta\Lambda}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].\end{split}

The first term above will be responsible for producing the
\mathscr{L}^{2}
term on the right side of
eq.

3.38
. We start by writing

\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}(s+\varepsilon-\tau(x))|\Delta_{\tau(x)}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{s\leqslant\tau\leqslant s+\varepsilon}(s+\varepsilon-\tau)|\Delta_{\tau}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\right],

where on the right side, the sum is over all
\tau
’s that are jump discontinuites for
({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)_{s\geqslant 0}
. Recalling the definition of the bracket process
([{\mathsf{A}}u]_{s})_{s\geqslant 0}
defined in
eq.

2.7
, we use Fubini’s lemma and the
\mathscr{L}^{2}
isometry
{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left[{\mathsf{A}}u\right]_{s}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}\right]}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{s\leqslant\tau\leqslant s+\varepsilon}(s+\varepsilon-\tau)|\Delta_{\tau}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{s\leqslant\tau\leqslant s+\varepsilon}\int_{s}^{s+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\tau\leqslant t\leqslant s+\varepsilon\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t|\Delta_{\tau}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{s}^{s+\varepsilon}\sum_{s\leqslant\tau\leqslant t}|\Delta_{\tau}({\mathsf{A}}u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{s}^{s+\varepsilon}\left(\left[{\mathsf{A}}u\right]_{t}-\left[{\mathsf{A}}u\right]_{s}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{s}^{s+\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{t}u)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}\right]\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\varepsilon}u)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}\right]\right).

Putting this estimate back into
eq.

3.42
, we conclude the estimating of the term
eq.

3.40
-c2, obtaining

\displaystyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:PerThreeTerms}-c2}\leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{\beta\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\varepsilon}u)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}\right]\right)+\frac{\beta\Lambda}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash Q_{s}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

By choosing
\beta=\varepsilon
, recalling
eq.

3.41
, and that
r\leqslant s-2
, we can combine this estimate with those of
eq.

3.40
-a,
eq.

3.40
-b, and
eq.

3.40
-c1 to get

\frac{\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\ \leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\varepsilon}u)^{2}\right]+2\Lambda\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}\backslash{Q_{r}}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We now proceed with a hole-filling argument: adding
2\Lambda\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]
to both sides of the equation above, and using Jensen’s inequality, we obtain

\frac{\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}\right]+(2\Lambda+1)\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\frac{\Lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\varepsilon}u)^{2}\right]+2\Lambda\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s+\varepsilon}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

Dividing both sides by
(2\Lambda+1)
, and setting
\theta^{\prime}:=\frac{2\Lambda}{2\Lambda+1}
, we obtain the desired inequality
eq.

3.38
.
∎

We remark that
eq.

3.38
does not imply directly
eq.

3.37
. For example, let
r>2
and we choose
s=2r
and
\varepsilon=r
in
eq.

3.38
, then with a normalization of volume we get

\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2r^{2}\rho|Q_{r}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[({\mathsf{A}}_{2r}u)^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{r}|}\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{2r,r}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{2r,r}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant 3^{d}\theta^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2r^{2}\rho|Q_{3r}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[({\mathsf{A}}_{3r}u)^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{3r}|}\int_{Q_{3r}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right).

Then another factor
3^{d}
will be added, and we typically do not have
3^{d}\theta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, since we recall that
\theta^{\prime}=\frac{2\Lambda}{2\Lambda+1}
.

Proof of
Proposition

3.9
.

We apply Lemma
3.11
iteratively, with very small increments of the volume. Let
\delta>0
to be fixed later, and choose
{s=(1+\delta)r},\varepsilon=\delta r
. For convenience, we assume that
r
is sufficiently large that

(3.43)

\displaystyle s=(1+\delta)r\geqslant r+2,\quad\text{that is}\quad r\geqslant 2\delta^{-1}.

Equation (
3.38
) and Jensen’s inequality give us that, provided
(1+2\delta)r\leqslant 3r
,

(3.44)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{r}|}\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{(1+\delta)r,\delta r}u)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{(1+\delta)r,\delta r}u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\widetilde{\theta}\left(\frac{1}{2(\delta r)^{2}\rho|Q_{(1+2\delta)r}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{(1+2\delta)r}|}\int_{Q_{(1+2\delta)r}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right),

with
\widetilde{\theta}=(1+2\delta)^{d}\theta^{\prime}
. We choose the constant
\delta>0
sufficiently small that
\widetilde{\theta}<1
. In order to obtain
eq.

3.37
, we will now apply
eq.

3.44
iteratively, from the cube
Q_{r}
to the larger cube
Q_{3r}
.

We give the details for this argument—see also
Figure

2
for an illustration. We plan to use
eq.

3.44

(N+1)
times, and and let
\delta\in(0,1)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy

(3.45)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}=(1+2\delta)^{d}\theta^{\prime}<1,\qquad(1+2\delta)^{N+1}=3.

Then we set the scale and the
\mathbf{a}
-harmonic functions in every scale

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r_{n}=(1+2\delta)^{n}r&0\leqslant n\leqslant N+1,\\
u_{N+1}=u&,\\
u_{n}={\mathsf{A}}_{(1+\delta)r_{n},\delta r_{n}}u_{n+1}&0\leqslant n\leqslant N.\end{array}\right.

We can prove by induction that
u_{n}\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{r_{n}})
under the condition
eq.

3.43
. Then for every
0\leqslant n\leqslant N
, we apply
eq.

3.44
from
u_{n}
on
Q_{r_{n}}
to
u_{n+1}
on
Q_{r_{n+1}}

(3.49)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{r_{n}}|}\int_{Q_{r_{n}}}\nabla u_{n}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u_{n}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\widetilde{\theta}\left(\frac{1}{2(\delta r_{n})^{2}\rho|Q_{(1+2\delta)r_{n}}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[(u_{n+1})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{r_{n+1}}|}\int_{Q_{r_{n+1}}}\nabla u_{n+1}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u_{n+1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\right).

Iterating on
eq.

3.49
until
u_{N+1}=u
on
Q_{3r}
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{r}|}\int_{Q_{r}}\nabla u_{0}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u_{0}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
\leqslant\left(\frac{3^{d}}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{N}(1+2\delta)^{-2n}\right)\frac{1}{(\delta r)^{2}\rho|Q_{3r}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u^{2}]+(\widetilde{\theta})^{N+1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{3r}|}\int_{Q_{3r}}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We notice that
u_{0}
can be seen as as a weighted sum of
{\mathsf{A}}_{s^{\prime}}u
, for scales
s^{\prime}
satisfying
{s^{\prime}\geqslant(1+\delta)r\geqslant r+2}
, by
eq.

3.43
. So we apply once Jensen’s inequality for
u_{0}
and obtain
eq.

3.37
by setting

C(d,\Lambda):=\frac{3^{d}}{2\delta^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{N}(1+2\delta)^{-2n},\qquad\theta:=(\widetilde{\theta})^{N+1}.\qed

Although we will not use this later, we now give more explicit estimates for the choice of the parameters in the proof above, resulting from the conditions listed in
eq.

3.43
and
eq.

3.45
. It suffices to pick an integer
N
larger than
\left\lfloor\frac{d\log 3}{\log\left(1+\frac{1}{2\Lambda}\right)}\right\rfloor
, and then in
eq.

3.45
use
\delta:=\frac{1}{2}(3^{\frac{1}{N+1}}-1)
to fix
\delta
, and in
eq.

3.43
we require
r\geqslant 2\delta^{-1}
, which gives the condition for the minimal scale
R_{0}
. A possible choice is the following

\begin{split}&N:=2\left\lfloor\frac{d\log 3}{\log\left(1+\frac{1}{2\Lambda}\right)}\right\rfloor+1,\quad\delta:=\frac{1}{2}(3^{\frac{1}{N+1}}-1)\simeq\frac{1}{8d\Lambda},\quad R_{0}:=2\delta^{-1}\simeq 16d\Lambda,\\
&\widetilde{\theta}:=\theta^{\prime}(1+2\delta)^{d}\simeq\left(1+\frac{1}{2\Lambda}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},\ \ \theta:=(\widetilde{\theta})^{N+1}\simeq 3^{-d},\ \ C:=\frac{3^{d}}{2\delta^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{N}(1+2\delta)^{-2n}\simeq 2^{8}3^{d}d^{3}\Lambda^{3}.\end{split}

Figure 2.
An illustration of the iterative argument for the proof of Proposition
3.9
. Since
Lemma

3.11
can imply
Proposition

3.9
only for a comparison from scale
r
to
(1+2\delta)r
with
\delta
very small, we add many intermediate scales
r_{n}=(1+2\delta)^{n}r
between
r
and
3r
.

4.
Subadditive quantities

We aim to adapt the strategy in
[
11
, Chapter 2]
for our model in continuum configuration space. In this section, we define several subadditive quantities, denoted by
\nu,\nu^{*},J
, and develop their elementary properties. We then we use them and a renormalization argument to obtain a quantitative rate of convergence for
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
in
Section

5
.

4.1.
Subadditive quantities
\nu
and
\nu^{*}

For every bounded domain
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we define the affine function in
U
with slope
p
by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\ell_{p,U}(\mu):=\int_{U}p\cdot x\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x),

and introduce the subadditive quantities

(4.2)

\begin{split}\nu(U,p)&:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,U}+\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right],\\
\nu^{*}(U,q)&:=\sup_{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].\end{split}

The quantity
\nu
can be thought of as the average energy per unit volume of the solution which matches with the behavior of the affine function
\ell_{p,U}
when a particle leaves the domain
U
. The quantity
\nu^{*}
is analogous to a Neumann problem with prescribed average flux of
q
. As will be seen below, the quantities
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
are approximately dual to one another; the quality of this approximation as the domain
U
grows to
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
will be central to the proof of Theorem
2.1
. If the matrix
\mathbf{a}
were constant, then by
eq.

2.6
the minimizer for
\nu(U,p)
would be
\ell_{p,U}
, and we would have
\nu(U,p)=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot\mathbf{a}p
; and similarly, were
\mathbf{a}
constant, we would have
\nu^{*}(U,q)=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot\mathbf{a}^{-1}q
.

We start by recording elementary properties satisfied by
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
. We recall that
\mathcal{G}_{U}=\sigma(\mu(U),\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}\backslash U))
. For every
r>0
, we denote by
B_{r}(U)
the
r
-enlargement of
U
, that is,
B_{r}(U):=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}:\dist(x,U)<r\}
.

Proposition 4.1

(Elementary properties of
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
)
.

The following properties hold for every bounded domain
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with Lipschitz boundary and
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

(1) There exists a unique solution for the optimization problem in the definition of
\nu(U,p)
that satisfies
{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[v-\ell_{p,U}]=0}
; we denote it by
v(\cdot,U,p)
. For the optimization problem in the definition of
\nu^{*}(U,q)
, there exists a maximizer
u(\cdot,U,q)
that is
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}
-measurable and such that
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u\,|\,\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}_{U}]=0
. They are
\mathbf{a}
-harmonic functions on
U
, i.e.
v(\cdot,U,p),u(\cdot,U,q)\in\mathcal{A}(U)
.

(2) There exist two
d\times d
symmetric matrices
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)
and
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)
such that

(4.3)

\nu(U,p)=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p,\qquad\nu^{*}(U,q)=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q,

and these matrices satisfy
{\mathsf{Id}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}}
and
{\mathsf{Id}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}}
.
Moreover,

(4.4)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}p^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right],

(4.5)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}q^{\prime}\cdot\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right].

(3) Slope:
v(\mu,U,p)
satisfies

(4.6)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{U}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{U}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]=p.

For the function
u(\cdot,U,q)
, there exists a
d\times d
symmetric matrix
{\mathsf{Id}\leqslant\mathbf{a}_{*}(U;\mathcal{G}_{U})\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}}
such that

(4.7)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{U}\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{\mathcal{G}}_{U}\right]=\mathbf{a}_{*}^{-1}(U;\mathcal{G}_{U})q,

and
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)=\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\mathbf{a}_{*}^{-1}(U;\mathcal{G}_{U})\mu(U)]
, so that

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]={\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q.

(4) Quadratic response: for every
v^{\prime}\in\ell_{p,U}+\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
, we have

(4.9)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla(v^{\prime}-v(\mu,U,p))\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla(v^{\prime}-v(\mu,U,p))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]-\nu(U,p).

Similarly, for every
u^{\prime}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
, we have

(4.10)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla(u^{\prime}-u(\mu,U,q))\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla(u^{\prime}-u(\mu,U,q))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=\nu^{*}(U,q)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u^{\prime}+q\cdot\nabla u^{\prime}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

(5) The quantities
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
are subadditive: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.11)

\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p)\leqslant\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p),\qquad\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)\leqslant\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},q).

Proof.

We prove each of these points in turn.

(1)We study at first the maximizer for the problem
\nu^{*}(U,q)
. A first observation is that the maximizer can be found in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}
-measurable functions. Because for any
u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
, its conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]
reaches a larger value for the functional in
\nu^{*}(U,q)
. We use Jensen’s inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]+q\cdot\nabla\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\nabla u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]\cdot\mathbf{a}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\nabla u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]+q\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\nabla u\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}]\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(U)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

By a variational calculus, we know the characterization of a maximizer with elliptic equation that for any
\phi\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}q\cdot\nabla\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

Similarly to the discussion in the proof of
Proposition

3.4
, we know that a solution for this problem also satisfies the more precise equation

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\Big|\,\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}_{U}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}q\cdot\nabla\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\Big|\,\mathcal{G}_{U}\right],

and we can define its solution in the space

\displaystyle W=\{f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U):\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[f\,|\,\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}_{U}]=0\}.

In this space, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[f^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}_{U}]\leqslant C\diam(U)^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}|\nabla f|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\big|\,\mathcal{G}_{U}\right],

by the Poincaré inequality
Proposition

3.2
. Then the coercivity on left hand side in
eq.

4.13
is ensured and we can apply the Lax-Milgram theorem. We call this maximizer
u(\mu,U,q)
. Testing
eq.

4.12
with
\phi\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
,
eq.

2.6
implies that its right hand side is
0
, so we have
u(\mu,U,q)\in\mathcal{A}(U)
.

Then we turn to
\nu(U,p)
. By a first order variation calculus, we know that a minimizer
v
for
\nu(U,p)
is characterized by an elliptic equation that for any
\phi\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla(v-\ell_{p,U})\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We remark that one cannot treat this equation as
eq.

4.12
, because
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[v|\mathcal{G}_{U}]
is not an element in
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
and we cannot subtract it. On the other hand, we can apply the Lax-Milgram theorem on the space

\displaystyle V=\{f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U):\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[f]=0\},

to define the unique solution
v-\ell_{p,U}\in V
. We notice that the right hand side of
eq.

4.14
is clearly a bounded linear functional, and the coercivity of the left hand side of
eq.

4.14
is ensured by the Poincaré inequality
Proposition

3.3
on
V
. We denote this minimizer by
v(\mu,U,p)
, and
eq.

4.14
implies that
v(\mu,U,p)\in\mathcal{A}(U)
.

(2) We test at first
eq.

4.14
with
{v(\mu,U,p^{\prime})-\ell_{p^{\prime},U}\in\mathscr{H}_{0}^{1}(U)}
and obtain that

(4.15)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)p^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right],

and this implies
{(p,p^{\prime})\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]}
is a bilinear map
p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p^{\prime}
. This definition with
eq.

4.15

proves
eq.

4.4
. We let
p=p^{\prime}
and obtain that
\nu(U,p)=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p
. To obtain the bound of
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)
, we use the bound of
\mathbf{a}
and the definition of
eq.

4.2

\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,U}+\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\leqslant\nu(U,p)=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p\\
\leqslant\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,U}+\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{\Lambda}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We can check that
\ell_{p,U}
is the minimizer for
\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,U}+\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{\Lambda}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right],

then it concludes the proof of the bound
{\mathsf{Id}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}}
.

The same argument works for
\nu^{*}(U,q)
. We test
eq.

4.12
with
u(\mu,U,q^{\prime})
and obtain that

(4.16)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}q\cdot\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right].

This proves that
(q,q^{\prime})\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q^{\prime})\right]
is also bilinear and we denote it by
q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q^{\prime}
, and this also concludes
eq.

4.5
. Then we put
q^{\prime}=q
and
eq.

4.16
in the definition of
eq.

4.2
that

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(U,q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)+q\cdot\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla u(\mu,x,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q.

This proves the bilinear map expression for
\nu^{*}(U,q)
. Concerning the bound for the matrix
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)
, we use the bound for
\mathbf{a}
and the equations above to obtain that

(4.17)

\sup_{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{\Lambda}{2}|\nabla u|^{2}+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\leqslant\nu^{*}(U,q)\\
\leqslant\sup_{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u|^{2}+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

One can check for the lower bound,
\ell_{\frac{q}{\Lambda},U}
attains the maximum and for the upper bound it is
\ell_{q,U}
that attains the maximum. Then we put the expression
\nu^{*}(U,q)=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q
and obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda^{-1}}{2}|q|^{2}\leqslant\nu^{*}(U,q)=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q\leqslant\frac{1}{2}|q|^{2},

which implies the bound for
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)
.

(3) The slope identity
eq.

4.6
for
v(\mu,U,p)
is directly the result from
eq.

2.6
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{U}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\ \Bigg|\ \mu(U)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{U}p\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mu(U)\right]=p.

For the function
u(\mu,U,q)
, the identity
eq.

4.8
comes directly from
eq.

4.5
, but conditioned
\mathcal{G}_{U}
, the averaged slope is not
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q
. In fact, we recall that
u(\mu,U,q)
is also the conditioned maximizer for
eq.

4.13
, so we can define the matrix
{\mathbf{a}_{*}^{-1}(U;\mathcal{G}_{U})}
, the quenched slope
eq.

4.7
. The estimate for this matrix is then obtained by repeating the argument in
eq.

4.3
for
eq.

4.13
.

(4) We test
eq.

4.14
with
(v^{\prime}-\ell_{p,U})
and put it in the left hand side of
eq.

4.9

(4.18)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla(v^{\prime}-v(\cdot,U,p))\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla(v^{\prime}-v(\cdot,U,p))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
&\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla v^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
&\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].\end{split}

The term
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]
also appears on the right side of
eq.

4.4
with
p=p^{\prime}
, thus we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right],

and we put it back to
eq.

4.18
to conclude for the validity of
eq.

4.9
.

Similarly, we develop the left hand side of
eq.

4.10
as
eq.

4.18
, and use
eq.

4.12
with
\phi=u^{\prime}
to treat the inner product term of
u^{\prime}
and
u(\cdot,U,q)
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u(\cdot,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot q\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We put this term in the left hand side of
eq.

4.10
and use the bilinear map expression of
\nu^{*}(U,q)
to obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla(u^{\prime}-u(\cdot,U,q))\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla(u^{\prime}-u(\cdot,U,q))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla u(\cdot,U,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u(\cdot,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u(\cdot,U,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]+\nu^{*}(U,q)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla u^{\prime}\cdot q\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

This concludes the proof of
eq.

4.10
.

(5) For
\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p)
, we test the associated variational problem with the candidate
v^{\prime}=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n+1,n}}v(\cdot,z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p)
, which is an element of
\ell_{p,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1}}+\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1})
, so that

\displaystyle\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1}}\nabla v^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=3^{-d}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n+1,n}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla v(\cdot,z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p).

In the last step, we also use the stationarity of the coefficient field
\mathbf{a}
.

For
\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p)
, we also use that, for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n+1,n}
, we have the inclusion
{\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1})\subseteq\mathscr{H}^{1}(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})}
, so its unit energy on every small cube
z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}
is less than the maximum
\nu^{*}(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p)
, thus

\displaystyle\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3^{-d}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n+1,n}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\int_{z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)+q\cdot\nabla u(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 3^{-d}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n+1,n}}\nu^{*}(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},q).\qed

4.2.
Subadditive quantity
J

We now study the quantity
J
defined by

(4.19)

\begin{split}J(U,p,q):&=\nu(U,p)+\nu^{*}(U,q)-p\cdot q\\
&=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p+\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q-p\cdot q.\end{split}

By the properties of
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
, the quantity
J
is also subadditive. We briefly explain why this quantity will be convenient for our purposes. If the functions
\nu(U,\cdot)
and
\nu^{*}(U,\cdot)
were exactly convex dual of one another, then we would have that
J\geqslant 0
and that for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, the infimum of
J(U,p,\cdot)
is zero. This would correspond to the situation in which
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)
and
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)
are equal, and for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we would in fact have that
J(U,p,{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p)=0
. Instead, we will show below that, for any symmetric matrix
\mathsf{Id}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}
, we have

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)|+|\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}-\mathbf{a}_{*}(U)|\leqslant\sup_{p\in B_{1}}C(J(U,p,\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}p))^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The right side of the inequality above can be thought of as a measure of the defect in the convex duality relationship between
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
. For
U={\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
and using
\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}={\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
, we obtain that

\displaystyle|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})|\leqslant\sup_{p\in B_{1}}C(J(U,p,{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})p))^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since we know that
\{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\}_{m\geqslant 0}
is a decreasing sequence while
\{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\}_{m\geqslant 0}
is a increasing sequence from
eq.

4.3
and
eq.

4.11
, each sequence has a limit.
Therefore, once we prove a rate of convergence to zero for
J(U,p,{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})p)
, we get that the two limits coincide, and also a rate for the convergence of
\{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\}_{m\geqslant 0}
.

The rest of this section will present this strategy in details. We establish at first a variational description for the quantity
J
and the properties mentioned above.

Lemma 4.2
.

(1) For every
p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we have the variational representation

(4.20)

J(U,p,q)=\sup_{w\in\mathcal{A}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla w\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w+q\cdot\nabla w\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

(2) We have that
J(U,p,q)\geqslant 0
and
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)\geqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)
.

(3) There exists a constant
C(d,\Lambda)<\infty
such that and for every symmetric matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}
satisfying
{\mathsf{Id}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}}
, we have

(4.21)

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)|+|\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)|\leqslant C\sup_{p\in B_{1}}(J(U,p,\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}p))^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

(1) We start by rewriting the expression of
J(U,p,q)
using the definition of
\nu^{*}(U,q)
and the quadratic expression of
\nu(U,p)
. Noting also that the maximizer of
\nu^{*}(U,q)
belongs to
\mathcal{A}(U)
, we can write

(4.22)

J(U,p,q)=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
+\sup_{u\in\mathcal{A}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]-p\cdot q.

We claim that for any
{u\in\mathcal{A}(U)}
, with
{w:=u-v(\cdot,U,p)}
, we have

(4.23)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]-p\cdot q\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla w\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w+q\cdot\nabla w\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

To prove it, we can develop the right hand side of
eq.

4.23

(4.24)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla w\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w+q\cdot\nabla w\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]-p\cdot q\\
&\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla\left(v(\cdot,U,p)-\ell_{p,U}\right)\cdot\left(\mathbf{a}\nabla u-\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p)-q\ \right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].\\
\end{split}

Because
\left(v(\cdot,U,p)-\ell_{p,U}\right)\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
, we apply
u,v(\cdot,U,p)\in\mathcal{A}(U)
and
eq.

2.6
, the last line of
eq.

4.24
is
0
and we prove
eq.

4.23
. Then we take the maximum as
eq.

4.22
and obtain the definition
eq.

4.20
.

(2) The properties that
J(U,p,q)\geqslant 0
comes from the definition of
\nu^{*}(U,q)
: we test the functional in the definition of
\nu^{*}(U,q)
with the minimizer
v(\cdot,U,p)
of
\nu(U,p)
and obtain that

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(U,q)

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)+q\cdot\nabla v(\mu,x,U,p)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\quad=p\cdot q-\nu(U,p),

so that

J(U,p,q)=\nu(U,p)+\nu^{*}(U,q)-p\cdot q\geqslant 0.

Then we test
J(U,p,q)\geqslant 0
with that
q={\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)p
and obtain that

\displaystyle 0\leqslant J(U,p,{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p+\frac{1}{2}({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)p)\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)p)-p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)p,

and therefore
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)\geqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)
.

(3)
Using this property, we have

\displaystyle J(U,p,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p+\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q-p\cdot q

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p+\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(U)q-p\cdot q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p-q){\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(U)\cdot({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)p-q).

We put
q=\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}p
and obtain
|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)-\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}|\leqslant C\sup_{p\in B_{1}}(J(U,p,\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}p))^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The proof of the statement concerning
|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)-\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}|
is similar.
∎

In view of the definition of
J
, this functional enjoys properties similar to those described in
Proposition

4.1
for
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
.

Proposition 4.3

(Elementary properties of
J
)
.

For every bounded domain
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with Lipschitz boundary and
p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, the quantity
J(U,p,q)
defined in
eq.

4.19
satisfies the following properties:

(1) Characterization of optimizer: the optimization problem in
eq.

4.20
admits a unique solution
v(\cdot,U,p,q)\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[v(\cdot,U,p,q)\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{U}]=0
. This solution is such that for every
w\in\mathcal{A}(U)
,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\left(-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w+q\cdot\nabla w\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right],

and
(p,q)\mapsto v(\cdot,U,p,q)
a linear map. The function
v(\cdot,U,p,q)
can be expressed in terms of the optimizers in
eq.

4.2
as

(4.26)

\displaystyle v(\mu,U,p,q)=u(\mu,U,q)-v(\mu,U,p)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u(\mu,U,q)-v(\mu,U,p)\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{U}].

We have the quadratic expression

(4.27)

\displaystyle J(U,p,q)=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

(2) Slope:
v(\cdot,U,p,q)
satisfies

(4.28)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\strokedint_{U}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{G}_{U}\right]&=\mathbf{a}^{-1}_{*}(U;\mathcal{G}_{U})q-p,\\
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]&={\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U)q-p,\end{split}

where the matrix
\mathbf{a}^{-1}_{*}(U;\mathcal{G}_{U})
is defined in
eq.

4.7
.

(3) Quadratic response: for every
w\in\mathcal{A}(U)
, we have

(4.29)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(\frac{1}{2}\nabla(w-v(\cdot,U,p,q))\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla(w-v(\cdot,U,p,q))\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=J(U,p,q)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla w\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w+q\cdot\nabla w\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

(4) Subadditivity: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(4.30)

\displaystyle J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p,q)\leqslant J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,q).

Proof.

(1) The equation
eq.

4.25
comes directly from the first order variation calculus. The proof of the existence and uniqueness of the solution
v(\cdot,U,p,q)
is similar as the one for
\nu^{*}(U,q)
.
Equation

4.25
also implies that the map
(p,q)\mapsto v(\cdot,U,p,q)
is linear because for any
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and any
w\in\mathcal{A}(U)
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p_{1}+p_{2},q_{1}+q_{2})\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}-(p_{1}+p_{2})\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w+(q_{1}+q_{2})\cdot\nabla w\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{U}\nabla(v(\cdot,U,p_{1},q_{1})+v(\cdot,U,p_{2},q_{2}))\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla w\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

Then
(v(\cdot,U,p_{1},q_{1})+v(\cdot,U,p_{2},q_{2}))
is also a solution for the problem
eq.

4.25
with parameter
(p_{1}+p_{2},q_{1}+q_{2})
. Notice that we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[(v(\cdot,U,p_{1},q_{1})+v(\cdot,U,p_{2},q_{2}))\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{U}]=0,

it implies
v(\mu,U,p_{1}+p_{2},q_{1}+q_{2})=v(\mu,U,p_{1},q_{1})+v(\mu,U,p_{2},q_{2})
and the linearity of the map.

The exact expression of
v(\mu,U,P,q)
comes from the equivalent definition
eq.

4.20
of
J(U,p,q)
and its proof. We put
v(\mu,U,p,q)
in the first order variation
eq.

4.25

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\left(-p\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)+q\cdot\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].
