\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{5}{9}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n-2}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n-2}}|\mathsf{S}_{n-2}\nabla v({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n-2})-({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n-2}^{-1}q-p)|^{2}\,d\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{5}{9}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\right)V_{n-2},

where we recall that
V_{n}
is the left hand side of
eq.

5.13
. We put this estimate together with
eq.

5.15
, (
5.16
), (
5.20
) back to
eq.

5.14
to obtain the recurrence relation

\displaystyle(V_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant\left(\frac{5}{9}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(V_{n-2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+C(\tau_{n-2}+\tau_{n-1})^{\frac{1}{2}}+C3^{-dn}.

By choosing
\delta(d)>0
sufficiently small, we obtain the desired result
eq.

5.13
.
∎

Figure 3.
In the cube
\square_{n}
and all its sub-cubes
\{z+\square_{n-2}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,n-2}}
, for a chosen sub-cube
z_{0}+\square_{n-2}
(the cube in dark red), the support of
{v(z_{0}+\square_{n-2})}
is in
z_{0}+\square_{n-1}
(the cube in light red), so it has at most
5^{d}
cubes of scale
3^{n-2}
whose associated function has a support intersecting with
z_{1}+\square_{n-1}
(the cube in blue). For example,
v(z_{2}+\square_{n-2})
has correlation with
v(z_{0}+\square_{n-2})
, while
{v(z_{1}+\square_{n-2})},{v(z_{3}+\square_{n-2})}
do not. This gives us the contraction factor
\left(\frac{5}{9}\right)^{d}
.

5.4.
Step 4: iterations

Once we obtain the estimate
eq.

5.9
, it remains to do some numerical iterations, similarly to
[
11
, Page 59-60]
. For the reader’s convenience, we recall the main steps here. Let
\{\mathbf{e}_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}
denote the canonical basis in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and define

\displaystyle F_{m}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\mathbf{e}_{i},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{m}\mathbf{e}_{i}).

In order to obtain an exponential decay for
(F_{m})_{m\geqslant 0}
, we first introduce a weighted version of this quantity:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{m}:=\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-n)}F_{n}.

Here the exponent
\beta
is the same as in
eq.

5.9
. It is clear that
F_{m}\leqslant\widetilde{F}_{m}
, so it suffices to prove an exponential decay for
(\widetilde{F}_{m})_{m\geqslant 0}
. We will do so by proving a recurrence equation of type
\widetilde{F}_{m+1}\leqslant C(\widetilde{F}_{m}-\widetilde{F}_{m+1})
for some constant
C(d,\Lambda)<\infty
. Thus in the following, we calculate some bounds for
(\widetilde{F}_{m}-\widetilde{F}_{m+1})
and
\widetilde{F}_{m+1}
.

Starting with
(\widetilde{F}_{m}-\widetilde{F}_{m+1})
, we write

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{m}-\widetilde{F}_{m+1}\geqslant\sum_{n=0}^{n}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-n)}(F_{n}-F_{n+1})-C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}}.

Noticing that
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n+1}p
is the minimizer for the mapping
{q\mapsto J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p,q)}
in
eq.

4.19
, we have

(5.23)

\displaystyle F_{n+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},\mathbf{e}_{i},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n+1}\mathbf{e}_{i})\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},\mathbf{e}_{i},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{i}).

Using also
eq.

4.19
, that
{\mathsf{Id}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}\leqslant\Lambda\mathsf{Id}}
, and that
p\mapsto\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p)-\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p)
and
q\mapsto\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},q)-\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)
are positive semidefinite quadratic forms, we get

\displaystyle F_{n}-F_{n+1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}(J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},\mathbf{e}_{i},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{i})-J({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},\mathbf{e}_{i},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},\mathbf{e}_{i})-\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},\mathbf{e}_{i}))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{i})-\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{i}))

\displaystyle\geqslant C^{-1}\left(\sup_{p\in B_{1}}\left(\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p)-\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p)\right)+\sup_{q\in B_{1}}\left(\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},q)-\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},q)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant C^{-1}\tau_{n},

and thus

(5.24)

\widetilde{F}_{m}-\widetilde{F}_{m+1}\geqslant C^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-n)}\tau_{n}-C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}}.

For the upper bound of
\widetilde{F}_{m+1}
, we use
eq.

5.23
to see that
F_{n}\leqslant F_{n+1}
, so

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{m+1}

\displaystyle=3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m+1)}F_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-n)}F_{n+1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}}+\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-n)}F_{n}.

Then we apply
eq.

5.9
into the result above to get

(5.25)

\begin{split}\widetilde{F}_{m+1}&\leqslant C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}}+\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-n)}\left(3^{-\beta n}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}3^{-\beta(n-k)}\tau_{k}\right)\\
&\leqslant C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}}+3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}m}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\tau_{k}\sum_{n=k}^{m}3^{\frac{\beta}{2}(2k-n)}\\
&\leqslant C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}}+C\sum_{k=0}^{m}3^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(m-k)}\tau_{k}.\end{split}

We combine
eq.

5.24
and
eq.

5.25
, to obtain
C(\widetilde{F}_{m}-\widetilde{F}_{m+1}+\widetilde{C}3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}})\geqslant\widetilde{F}_{m+1}
, which implies

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{m+1}\leqslant\theta\widetilde{F}_{m}+C3^{-\frac{\beta m}{2}},

for some
\theta(d,\Lambda)\in(0,1)
. We thus conclude for the exponential decay of
(\widetilde{F}_{m})_{m\geqslant 0}
, and thus also of
F_{m}
, since
F_{m}\leqslant\widetilde{F}_{m}
. By
eq.

4.21
, this completes the proof of
Theorem

2.1
.

Appendix A
Some elementary properties of the function spaces

Lemma A.1

(Canonical projection)
.

Let
f:\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}
be a function, and for every Borel set
U
, measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f_{n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
denote the (permutation-invariant) function

f_{n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}):\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}U^{n}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\mapsto&f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}\right).\end{array}\right.

The following statements are equivalent.

(1) The function
f
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

(2) For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{B}_{U}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{U^{c}}
-measurable.

Proof.

We start from
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
. Because
{\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{U}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{U^{c}}}
, it suffices to study the product function

\displaystyle{f=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(V_{1})=n_{1}\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(V_{2})=n_{2}\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(U)=n\right\}}},

for some Borel sets
{V_{1}\subseteq U},{V_{2}\subseteq U^{c}}
. In this case, we have

\displaystyle\{f_{n}=1\}

\displaystyle=\{\mu(V_{1})=n_{1}\}\cap\{\mu(V_{2})=n_{2}\}\cap\{\mu(U)=n\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\{x_{\sigma(i)}\in V_{1}\}\bigcap_{j={n_{1}+1}}^{n}\{x_{\sigma(j)}\in(U\backslash V_{1})\}\bigcap\{\mu(V_{2})=n_{2}\}\right),

where
S_{n}
is the symmetric group. This proves that
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{B}_{U}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{U^{c}}
-measurable.

We turn to
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
. Let us pick a suitable
f_{n}
and
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
, then the main point is to establish the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable property. Since
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{B}_{U}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{U^{c}}
-measurable and permutation-invariant, it suffices to study the function of type

(A.1)

\displaystyle f_{n}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{\sigma(i)}\in V_{i}\right\}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=3.01389pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.3014pt\vrule height=0.3014pt,depth=0.0pt,width=2.1097pt}U^{c}(V_{0})=n_{0}\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(U)=n\right\}},

for
\{V_{i}\}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n}
Borel sets. This is still a complicated function, but we can add one more condition

(A.2)

\displaystyle\forall 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n,\quad V_{i}=V_{j}\text{ or }V_{i}\cap V_{j}=\emptyset.

For example, let
\{\widetilde{V}_{j}\}_{0\leqslant j\leqslant m}
be all the different elements in
\{V_{i}\}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n}
, and
\widetilde{V}_{j}
appears
n_{j}
times. For the functions of type
eq.

A.1
satisfying the condition
eq.

A.2
, the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable property is easy to treat since we have

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{\sigma(i)}\in V_{i}\right\}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=3.01389pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.3014pt\vrule height=0.3014pt,depth=0.0pt,width=2.1097pt}U^{c}(V_{0})=n_{0}\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(U)=n\right\}}\\
=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\widetilde{V}_{j})=n_{j}\right\}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=3.01389pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.3014pt\vrule height=0.3014pt,depth=0.0pt,width=2.1097pt}U^{c}(V_{0})=n_{0}\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(U)=n\right\}},

which is an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function.

Finally, let us conclude that for a general
f_{n}
in
eq.

A.1
, they can be decomposed into the sum of the functions with the propriety
eq.

A.2
. Let us see the case
n=2
, where we have the following decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in V_{1}\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in V_{2}\right\}}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in(V_{1}\backslash V_{2})\right\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in(V_{1}\cap V_{2})\right\}})(\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in(V_{2}\backslash V_{1})\right\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in(V_{1}\cap V_{2})\right\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in(V_{1}\backslash V_{2})\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in(V_{2}\backslash V_{1})\right\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in(V_{1}\backslash V_{2})\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in(V_{1}\cap V_{2})\right\}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in(V_{1}\cap V_{2})\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in(V_{2}\backslash V_{1})\right\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in(V_{1}\cap V_{2})\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{2}\in(V_{1}\cap V_{2})\right\}}.

For a general
n
, one can use induction and this concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

For every
s>0
and
f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})
, we have
{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})
, and for every
x\in\supp(\mu)\cap Q_{s}

(A.3)

\displaystyle\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s}f)(\mu,x)={\mathsf{A}}_{s}(\nabla f)(\mu,x).

Moreover, if
s>2
and
f\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{s})
, then
{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{s-2})
.

Proof.

At first, we should remark the well-definedness of the right side of
eq.

A.3
. Notice that the Poisson measure can be decomposed as a sum of the independent parts
{\mu=\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime}).

Thus the right-hand side of
eq.

A.3
is defined as

(A.4)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{s}(\nabla f)(\mu,x):=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\nabla f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c},x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime}).

We prove
eq.

A.3
and
{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})
for the functions in
{\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Q_{s})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})}
as they are dense, and we can focus on the case
\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n
fixed. We use Lemma
A.1
to write

f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}\right)=f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}).

Following the property of product measure, for every
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n})\in(\overline{Q}_{s})^{n}
, the mapping

\displaystyle{\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}\mapsto f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})},

is
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}}
-measurable. Thus for every
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n})\in(Q_{s})^{n}
, the mapping

\displaystyle{\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}\mapsto\nabla_{x_{k}}f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})},

is also
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}}
-measurable because it is the limit of
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}}
-measurable functions. Then we observe that

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{(Q_{s})^{n}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\nabla_{x_{k}}f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\mathbb{Q}\cap Q_{s})^{n}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\nabla_{x_{k}}f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

as a supremum of a countable number of
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}}
-measurable functions, is finite and
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{Q}_{s}^{c}}
-measurable. Thus we can define a cut-off version of
f
that

\displaystyle f^{n,M}=f\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}\leqslant M\right\}},

and we can establish
eq.

A.3
at first for
f^{n,M}
. For every
x\in Q_{s}\cap\supp(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{k}({\mathsf{A}}_{s}f^{n,M})(\mu,x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\frac{f((\mu-\delta_{x}+\delta_{x+h\mathbf{e}_{k}})\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})-f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})}{h}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}\leqslant M\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n\right\}},

for
h
small enough such that
x+h\mathbf{e}_{k}\in Q_{s}
. Since
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Q_{s})
, we use
Lemma

A.1
and the mean value theorem

\displaystyle\frac{f(\mu-\delta_{x}+\delta_{x+h\mathbf{e}_{k}})-f(\mu)}{h}=\partial_{k}f(\mu-\delta_{x}+\delta_{x+\theta\mathbf{e}_{k}},x+\theta\mathbf{e}_{k}),

for some
\theta\in(0,1)
. With the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}\leqslant M\right\}}
, this term is bounded by
M
, so we can use the dominated convergence theorem that

\displaystyle\partial_{k}({\mathsf{A}}_{s}f^{n,M})(\mu,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{f((\mu-\delta_{x}+\delta_{x+h\mathbf{e}_{k}})\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})-f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c})}{h}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}\leqslant M\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n\right\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\partial_{k}f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c},x)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}\leqslant M\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n\right\}},

which establishes the
eq.

A.3
in the sense
eq.

A.4
. By Jensen’s inequality and Fubini’s lemma, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}|\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s}f^{n,M})|^{2}(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\left|\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\nabla f^{n,M}(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c},x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\left|\nabla f^{n,M}(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c},x)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\left|\nabla f^{n,M}\right|^{2}(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right],

which implies that
{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f^{n,M}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})
. Then we use once again Jensen’s inequality for
f^{n,M}
and
f^{n,M^{\prime}}
with
M<M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}|\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s}f^{n,M})-\nabla({\mathsf{A}}_{s}f^{n,M^{\prime}})|^{2}(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\left|\nabla f^{n,M}-\nabla f^{n,M^{\prime}}\right|^{2}(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\left|\nabla f\right|^{2}(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{M<\left\|{\nabla f_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((Q_{s})^{n})}\leqslant M^{\prime}\right\}}\right].

So
\{f^{n,M}\}_{M\geqslant 0}
gives a Cauchy sequence in
\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})
, and the only candidate is
f\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mu(\overline{Q}_{s})=n\right\}}
because it is the limit in
\mathscr{L}^{2}
. By this and a linear combination, we establish
eq.

A.3
for
f
in
{\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Q_{s})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})}
, and we can then extend to a general function in
\mathscr{H}^{1}(Q_{s})
by the density argument.

For the part of
\mathbf{a}
-harmonic function, we suppose
f\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{s})
and test
\phi\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(Q_{s-2})
with
eq.

A.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}(\nabla{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f)(\mu,x)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla\phi(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}{\mathsf{A}}_{s}(\nabla f)(\mu,x)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla\phi(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\nabla f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c},x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\right)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla\phi(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right].

Restricted on
x\in Q_{s-2}
, we have
\mathbf{a}(\mu,x),\nabla\phi(\mu,x)
are
\mathcal{F}_{Q_{s}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{Q_{s}}
-measurable, so we have

\displaystyle\forall x\in\supp(\mu)\cap Q_{s-2},\qquad\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla\phi(\mu,x)=\mathbf{a}(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s},x)\nabla\phi(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s},x).

We can enter the part in the integration, and then use Fubini’s lemma

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}(\nabla{\mathsf{A}}_{s}f)(\mu,x)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla\phi(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\nabla f(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}+\mu^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s}^{c},x)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s},x)\nabla\phi(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\overline{Q}_{s},x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\mu^{\prime})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s-2}}\nabla f(\mu,x)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,x)\nabla\phi(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=0.

In the last step, we use
f\in\mathcal{A}(Q_{s})
and this finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Equivalent definitions of the effective diffusion matrix

Recall that we defined
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U)
and
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U)
according to
eq.

4.1
-
eq.

4.3
. The proof of Theorem
2.1
ensures the existence of a constant
C<\infty
and an exponent
\alpha>0
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

(B.1)

\left|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}\right|+\left|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}\right|\leqslant C3^{-\alpha m}.

Throughout this appendix, we will only rely on the qualitative statement that

(B.2)

{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}).

The first main goal of this appendix is to demonstrate that the definition we chose for the bulk diffusion matrix indeed coincides with the “stationary” definition appearing in works such as
[
69
,
34
]
. Adapted to our context, this alternative definition takes the following form. For

(B.3)

\displaystyle\Gamma:=\left\{\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tau_{x}g(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\quad g\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\right\},

we let
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}
be the
d
-by-
d
matrix such that for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,

(B.4)

\displaystyle p\cdot\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}p:=\inf_{u\in\Gamma}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[(p+\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)(p+\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\right],

where in
eq.

B.3
, we used the notation
\tau_{x}g(\mu):=g(\tau_{-x}\mu)
. Notice that for
g\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
and
u:\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tau_{x}g(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\in\Gamma
, the function
\mu\mapsto u(\mu)
is typically not well-defined unless
\mu
is of finite support. However, the quantity
\nabla u(\mu,\cdot)
makes sense whenever the measure
\mu
is
\sigma
-finite, since in the sum
\nabla u(\mu,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x
, the function
g
is local, and thus the integrand
\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)
is non-zero only for
x
in a bounded set. With this interpretation of
\nabla u
, the right side of
eq.

B.4
is well-defined.

Theorem B.1
.

We have
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}=\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}
.

The second main goal of this appendix is to demonstrate that the infimum in
eq.

B.4
is achieved in a suitable completion of the space
\Gamma
. We also show that the optimizer, which we call the (stationary) corrector, can be obtained as a limit of approximations based on the finite-volume optimizers for
\nu
or
\nu^{*}
. Denoting

\mathcal{M}_{\bullet}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}):=\left\{(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ :\ x\in\supp\mu\right\},

we introduce the space

\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet}:=\left\{f:\mathcal{M}_{\bullet}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}\ :\ f\text{ is measurable and }\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]<\infty\right\},

and its local version

(B.5)

\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}:=\Big\{f:\mathcal{M}_{\bullet}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}\ :\ \mbox{$f$ is measurable and }\\
\mbox{for every compact $K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}$, }\ \mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{K}|f(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]<\infty\Big\}.

In these definitions, we say that
f:\mathcal{M}_{\bullet}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable provided that the mapping

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
(\mu,x)&\mapsto&f(\mu,x)\mathbf{1}_{\{x\in\supp\mu\}},\end{array}\right.

is
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}
-measurable. The space
\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}
is naturally endowed with the family of seminorms

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
f&\mapsto&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{K}|f(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{array}\right.

indexed by all the compact sets
K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. This family of seminorms turns
\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}
into a complete space.

For every
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\phi(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)
be such that the minimizer in the definition of
\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)
is
\ell_{p,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}+\phi(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)
, where we recall that
\ell_{p,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}
was defined in
eq.

4.1
. Similarly, we let
\phi^{*}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)
be such that
\ell_{p,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}+\phi^{*}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)
is the maximizer in the definition of
\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})p)
. More precisely, using the notation introduced in Proposition
4.1
, we have

v(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)=\ell_{p,U}+\phi(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p),

and

u(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})p)=\ell_{p,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}+\phi^{*}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p).

Finally, we define
\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}
according to the formula

(B.6)

\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}:\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\tau_{x}\phi(\mu,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\phi(\mu,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

Notice that, while
\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}(\mu)
is ill-defined when
\mu\sim\text{Poi}(\rho)
, the quantity
\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}(\mu,\cdot)
is still well-defined, for the same reason as in the discussion following
eq.

B.4
. Our second main result is as follows.

Theorem B.2
.

The following statements hold for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

(1) The sequence
(\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}})^{d}
. Its limit, which we denote by
\nabla\phi_{p}
, satisfies

(B.7)

\displaystyle\forall v\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}),\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla v\cdot\mathbf{a}(p+\nabla\phi_{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0.

(2) We have

(B.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}|\nabla\phi(\mu,\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)-\nabla\phi_{p}(\mu,\cdot)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0,

as well as

(B.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}|\nabla\phi^{*}(\mu,\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)-\nabla\phi_{p}(\mu,\cdot)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0.

(3) The effective diffusion matrix
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
satisfies

(B.10)

p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}p=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[(p+\nabla\phi_{p}(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)(p+\nabla\phi_{p}(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\right],

as well as

(B.11)

{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}p=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)(p+\nabla\phi_{p}(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\right].

As a preparation towards the proof of these results, we state in the following proposition a number of elementary properties about the function space
\Gamma
.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
g\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
be an
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}
-measurable function, and let
u:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tau_{x}g\,{\mathrm{d}}x\in\Gamma
. The following properties hold:

(1)

y\mapsto\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)
is a stationary field, i.e.
\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)=\nabla u(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{0},0)
.

(2)

\nabla u
has mean zero, that is,

(B.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right]=0.

(3)

\nabla u
satisfies the estimate

(B.13)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right]\\
\leqslant 3^{2dn}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla g(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla g(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

Proof.

(1) We use the definition to write

\displaystyle\partial_{k}u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tau_{x}g(\mu+\delta_{y+h\mathbf{e}_{k}})-\tau_{x}g(\mu+\delta_{y})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\left(\int_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\tau_{x}g(\mu+\delta_{y+h\mathbf{e}_{k}})-\tau_{x}g(\mu+\delta_{y})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\left(\int_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\tau_{x-y}g(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{h\mathbf{e}_{k}})-\tau_{x-y}g(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{0})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right).

From the first line to the second line, we used the fact that
g
is
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}
-measurable, so if the transport vector
x
does not belong to
y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}
, then the integrand vanishes (up to a boundary layer that vanishes in the limit
h\to 0
). We then do the change of variables
z=x-y
to get

\displaystyle\partial_{k}u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to\infty}\frac{1}{h}\left(\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\tau_{z}g(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{h\mathbf{e}_{k}})-\tau_{z}g(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{0})\,{\mathrm{d}}z\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{k}u(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{0},0),

which means that
y\mapsto\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)
is a stationary gradient field.

(2) We use the equations developed in the last question. Since
g\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, we can exchange the integration and derivative and get

(B.14)

\displaystyle\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla\tau_{z}g(\tau_{-y}\mu+\delta_{0},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}z.

We evaluate this gradient at
y=0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla\tau_{z}g(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}z\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla g(\tau_{-z}\mu+\delta_{-z},-z)\,{\mathrm{d}}z\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla g(\mu+\delta_{z},z)\,{\mathrm{d}}z\right]

\displaystyle=0.

From the second line to the third line, we used the stationarity of the Poisson point process. Because
g\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, then
\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla g(\mu+\delta_{z},z)\,{\mathrm{d}}z=0
and the integration in the third line vanishes.

(3) We pick a cube
Q_{L}=\left(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\right)^{d}
with
L>0
, make use of the stationarity of
{y\mapsto\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)}
and Mecke’s identity (see
[
52
, Theorem 4.1]
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{|Q_{L}|}\int_{Q_{L}}\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{y},y)\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)\,{\mathrm{d}}y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{L}|}\int_{Q_{L}}\nabla u(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla u(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

We put the definition
u=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tau_{x}g\,{\mathrm{d}}x
into the equation. For the gradient at
y
, as
g
is
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}
-measurable, thus only the term
\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)
for
x\in y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}
contributes. This gives

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|Q_{L}|}\int_{Q_{L}}\left(\int_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\left(\int_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

We next apply Jensen’s inequality to obtain that

(B.15)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|^{2}}{\rho|Q_{L}|}\int_{Q_{L}}\left(\strokedint_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\left(\strokedint_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
&\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|^{2}}{\rho|Q_{L}|}\int_{Q_{L}}\left(\strokedint_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
&\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}{\rho|Q_{L}|}\int_{Q_{L+3^{n}}}\left(\int_{x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right].\end{split}

In the last line, we use Fubini’s lemma and exchange
{\int(\cdots)\,{\mathrm{d}}x}
with
{\int(\cdots)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)}
. In this procedure, we have to enlarge the domain from
Q_{L}
to
Q_{L+3^{n}}
, because for the gradient at
y\in Q_{L}
,
\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)
contributes for the transport
x\in Q_{L+3^{n}}
(see
Figure

4
as an illustration). Using the stationarity of the Poisson point process, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla(\tau_{x}g)(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla g(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla g(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right],

which helps us conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[(\xi+\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)(\xi+\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 3^{2dn}\frac{|Q_{L+3^{n}}|}{|Q_{L}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla g(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\nabla g(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

We take
L\to\infty
and obtain the desired result.
∎

Remark B.4
.

The inequality
eq.

B.13
is essentially sharp when
\nabla g
itself is close to a stationary field. Indeed, if
g
is close to a stationary field, then

\displaystyle\nabla\tau_{x}g(\mu,y)=\nabla g(\tau_{-x}\mu,y-x)\simeq\nabla g(\mu,y),

which implies that the application of Jensen’s inequality in
eq.

B.15
is essentially sharp. The error introduced by a boundary layer in a subsequent step of the proof disappears as we take
L\to\infty
at the end.

As a corollary of
Proposition

B.3
, we can also propose the following equivalent definition of
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}
.

Corollary B.5
.

For any open set
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we have

(B.16)

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}\xi=\inf_{u\in\Gamma}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|U|}\int_{U}(\xi+\nabla u)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\xi+\nabla u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

Proof.

It is a direct result of Mecke’s identity (see
[
52
, Theorem 4.1]
) and the stationarity of
{y\mapsto\nabla u(\mu+\delta_{y},y)}
.
∎

Figure 4.
The red cube represents the cube
Q_{L}
and the blue cubes represent the transport of small cubes
x+\square_{n}
. It is clear that for
x\in Q_{L+3^{n}}
, the blue cubes and the red cube intersect.

With the help of
Proposition

B.3
, we can now prove the first main theorem of this appendix.

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

We decompose the proof into two steps.

Step 1: Bound from below
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}\geqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
.
We fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence of approximate minimizers
\{\phi_{{p}}^{(i)}\}_{i\geqslant 1}
for the variational problem in
eq.

B.4
, which we write in the form

\displaystyle\phi_{{p}}^{(i)}(\mu)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\tau_{x}g_{i}(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}x

for some
g_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
.
Now we propose a modified version in
\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)}(\mu):=\int_{K_{i}}\tau_{x}g_{i}(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

with
K_{i}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
a large compact set so that
\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
and

\displaystyle\forall y\in{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\qquad\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)}(\mu,y)=\nabla\phi_{{p}}^{(i)}(\mu,y).

Then we test
{p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)}
in the optimization problem for
\nu^{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q)
to get that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})q

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}({p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)})\cdot\mathbf{a}({p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)})+q\cdot({p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p}}^{(i)})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}({p}+\nabla\phi_{{p}}^{(i)})\cdot\mathbf{a}({p}+\nabla\phi_{{p}}^{(i)})+q\cdot({p}+\nabla\phi_{{p}}^{(i)})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We use the stationarity of
y\mapsto\nabla\phi_{{p}}^{(i)}(\mu+\delta_{y},y)
,
eq.

B.12
and let
i\to\infty
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})q\geqslant-\frac{1}{2}{p}\cdot\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}{p}+{p}\cdot q.

Taking
q={\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}){p}
leads to

\displaystyle{p}\cdot\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}{p}\geqslant{p}\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}){p}.

Finally, we let
m\to\infty
and conclude that
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}\geqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
.

Step 2: Bound from above
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
.
We hope to prove
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
by testing the variational formula
eq.

B.4
with a suitable candidate, namely the function
\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}
introduced in
eq.

B.6
.
Since
\phi(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
, and using
eq.

B.13
, we can approximate
\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}
in
(\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}})^{d}
arbitrarily closely with elements of
\Gamma
. It thus follows that we can use
\widetilde{\phi}_{p,m}
as a candidate in the variational problem in
eq.

B.4
, and use the comparison inequality
eq.

B.13
to get that

(B.17)

\displaystyle{p}\cdot\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}{p}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[({p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)({p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}(\mu+\delta_{0},0))\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}({p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p}))\cdot\mathbf{a}({p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]

\displaystyle={p}\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}){p}.

Finally, we let
m\to\infty
and conclude that
\widetilde{{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
.
∎

In the proof above, we used
\{\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}\}_{m\geqslant 1}
as a sequence of approximate minimizers for the variational problem in
eq.

B.4
. This already gives us a good hint for the validity of at least some of the statements in Theorem
B.2
. We now turn to the proof of the first part of this result.

Proof of part (1) of Theorem
B.2
.

We decompose the proof into four steps.

Step 1:
\{\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}\}_{m\geqslant 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
{(\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}})^{d}}
.

We fix
n<m
and, recalling the notation
\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}:=3^{n}{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\cap{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},n}(\mu,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla\tau_{x}\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

where the function
\phi_{{p},m,n}
is defined as

(B.18)

\displaystyle\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu):=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\phi(\mu,z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},{p}).

For any compact set
K
, we use Mecke’s identity (see
[
52
, Theorem 4.1]
) and the stationarity of
\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{K}|\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}-\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},n}|^{2}(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]

\displaystyle=\rho\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{K}|\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}-\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},n}|^{2}(\mu+\delta_{y},y)\,{\mathrm{d}}y\right]

\displaystyle=\rho|K|\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[|\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}-\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},n}|^{2}(\mu+\delta_{0},0)\right].

Then we use the comparison inequality
eq.

B.13
and obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{K}|\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}-\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},n}|^{2}(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho|K|\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})-\nabla\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu,y)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho|K|(\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},{p})-\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})).

By
eq.

B.2
, this shows that
\{\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m}\}_{m\geqslant 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
{(\mathscr{L}^{2}_{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}})^{d}}
.

Step 2: Harmonic property - setting up.
Denote the limit by
\nabla\phi_{p}
, we set things up to prove the harmonic property
eq.

B.7
by approximation. We fix
s>0
and
{v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}
which is
\mathcal{F}_{Q_{s}}
-measurable, and observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla v\cdot\mathbf{a}({p}+\nabla\phi_{{p}})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\nabla v\cdot\mathbf{a}({p}+\nabla\widetilde{\phi}_{{p},m})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}\nabla v(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\left(\strokedint_{y+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

We then use Fubini’s lemma to exchange the order of integration,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\nabla v\cdot\mathbf{a}({p}+\nabla\phi_{{p}})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{3^{m}+s}}\left(\int_{x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\nabla v(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\left({p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{y\in Q_{s}\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right].

For
m
sufficiently large, we can decompose the domain of integration in
x
in the expression above into
Q_{3^{m}-s}
and
Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}
. We analyse the contribution of each of these quantities in each of the following two steps.

Step 3: Integration in
Q_{3^{m}-s}
.
Notice that for
x\in Q_{3^{m}-s}
, we have
Q_{s}\subseteq x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
(see
Figure

5
for an illustration), thus we can drop the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\left\{y\in Q_{s}\right\}}
in the inner integral and use the
\mathbf{a}
-harmonic property of
{{p}+\nabla\phi(x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})}
to get that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{3^{m}-s}}\left(\int_{x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\nabla v(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\left({p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{Q_{3^{m}-s}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\nabla v(\mu,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(\mu,y)\left({p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\quad=0.

Figure 5.
The red cube represents
Q_{s}
, and the blue and green cubes represent respectively
Q_{3^{m}+s}
and
Q_{3^{m}-s}
. For
x\in Q_{3^{m}-s}
, we have
Q_{s}\subseteq x+\square_{m}
; for
x\notin Q_{3^{m}+s}
,
{(x+\square_{m})\cap Q_{s}=\emptyset}
; for
x\in Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}
,
x+\square_{m}
and
Q_{s}
have non-empty intersection but
Q_{s}
is not totally contained in
x+\square_{m}
. These three cases are represented by
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
.

Step 4: Boundary layer
Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}
.
We use Young’s inequality to bound the term with the integral over
x\in Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}
by

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}}\left(\int_{(x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\cap Q_{s}}|\nabla v(\mu,y)|^{2}+\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\underbrace{\frac{\Lambda|Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}|}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}|\nabla v(\mu,y)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]}_{\mathbf{(A)}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{\Lambda}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}}\left(\int_{(x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\cap Q_{s}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right]}_{\mathbf{(B)}}.

For the term
\mathbf{(A)}
, we have, for a constant
C
that may depend on
s
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{(A)}\leqslant C\Lambda 3^{-m}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{s}}|\nabla v(\mu,y)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{}0.

For the term
\mathbf{(B)}
, we use the stationarity to observe that

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{(x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\cap Q_{s}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,x+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}\cap(-x+Q_{s})}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

We apply once again Fubini’s lemma to
\mathbf{(B)}
and get that

\displaystyle\mathbf{(B)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}}\left(\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{y\in(-x+Q_{s})\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\left(\int_{Q_{3^{m}+s}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-s}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{y\in(-x+Q_{s})\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\Lambda|Q_{s}|}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\dist(y,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\leqslant s\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

That this term converges to zero is a consequence of the stronger estimate given by
Lemma

B.6
below. This concludes the proof for
{v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}
, and then we can use density argument to extend to general
{v\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}
.
∎

In the proof above, we appealed to the following boundary layer estimate, which we state as a separate lemma for future reference (and which is stronger than what was needed for the purpose of the proof above, since the boundary layer size is allowed to increase with
m
).

Lemma B.6

(Boundary layer estimate)
.

For every sequence
(s_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
s_{m}\leqslant 3^{m}
and
\lim_{m\to\infty}3^{-m}s_{m}=0
, we have

(B.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\dist(y,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\leqslant s_{m}\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]=0.

Proof.

The idea is to make use of the renormalization argument. We define a mesoscopic scale
n
associated to
m
such that
s_{m}\leqslant 3^{n}
,
n\to\infty
and
m-n\to\infty
. Then we immediately have

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\dist(y,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\leqslant s_{m}\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\dist(y,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\leqslant 3^{n}\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right].

We propose to compare
\phi(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)
with
\phi_{{p},m,n}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
defined as in
eq.

B.18
:

\displaystyle\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu)=\sum_{z\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\phi(\mu,z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},{p}).

Then we have

(B.20)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\dist(y,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\leqslant 3^{n}\right\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]\\
&\leqslant\underbrace{2\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{Q_{3^{m}}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-2\times 3^{n}}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu,y)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:BdDecom}-a}}\\
&\qquad+\underbrace{2\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{Q_{3^{m}}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-2\times 3^{n}}}\left|\nabla\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu,y)-\nabla\phi(\mu,y,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},{p})\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:BdDecom}-b}}.\end{split}

For the first term
eq.

B.20
-a, we do partition of sum into cubes of size
3^{n}
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\frac{1}{\rho|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{Q_{3^{m}}\backslash Q_{3^{m}-2\times 3^{n}}}\left|{p}+\nabla\phi_{{p},m,n}(\mu,y)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)\right]
